economy, the coastal lifestyle that Capetonians enjoy, and semigration (individuals moving from other South African provinces into the Western Cape).[[247]](#cite_note-What_the_average_house_price_in_South_Africa_gets_you_in_Joburg,_Durban_and_Cape_Town-249)[[248]](#cite_note-Super_luxury_home-sales_boom,_Joburg_buyers_pay_more_for_Cape_Town_houses-250)[[24]](#cite_note-CT-26)[[20]](#cite_note-South_Africans_flock_to_Cape_Town-22)\n\nCape Town trends among the top 20 cities worldwide for capital value growth, further increasing the attractiveness of owning a home in the city.[[250]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_foreign_buyer_hotspots_and_top_prices_paid_for_luxury_homes-252)\n\nCape Town housing is in short supply due to a number of issues, including [NIMBYISM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NIMBY \"NIMBY\"), delays in approvals for social housing, a lack of space to build outwards, and an increase in properties listed as [short-term rentals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-term_rental \"Short-term rental\") on [Airbnb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbnb \"Airbnb\") and similar platforms.[[237]](#cite_note-Dark_clouds_for_Cape_Town_property_and_its_residents-239)[[251]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_Southern_Suburbs:_Rising_prices_and_shorter_listing_times-253) The negative impact of the unchecked growth of short-term rentals, such as Airbnbs, on the housing market was confirmed in January 2026 by South African [Minister of Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Tourism_(South_Africa) \"Minister of Tourism (South Africa)\") [Patricia de Lille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricia_de_Lille \"Patricia de Lille\"). She stated that the primary solution was to build more affordable housing close to [Cape Town CBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_CBD \"Cape Town CBD\").[[252]](#cite_note-De_Lille_concedes_short-term_rentals_add_to_Cape_Town’s_housing_crisis-254)\n\nIn February 2026, the City of Cape Town announced plans to significantly increase [municipal rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rates_(tax) \"Rates (tax)\") for [short-term rental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-term_rental \"Short-term rental\") properties, as part of combating low housing availability. The shift will result in short-term rentals, like those from [Airbnb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbnb \"Airbnb\") and [Booking.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booking.com \"Booking.com\"), being charged commercial tariffs instead of residential ones. This would increase their tariffs by as much as 135%, and put them in the same category that [hotels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotel \"Hotel\") and [guest houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guest_house \"Guest house\") already are. As part of the announcement, the City stated it was correcting an imbalance in taxes, and that short-term rental operators should not be able to benefit from [residential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential \"Residential\") tariffs while running what is effectively a [commercial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce \"Commerce\") business.[[253]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_cracks_down_on_Airbnb_rates-255)\n\nDespite not being the [largest city in South Africa by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_South_Africa \"List of municipalities in South Africa\"), Cape Town has built the highest number of affordable housing units, by a significant margin. During the period 1994 through 2025, the City of Cape Town constructed a total of 202,070 affordable housing units. In February 2026, it was reported that during the preceding five years, Cape Town had handed over a total of 12,401 affordable houses (equating to 2,480 per year). This was more affordable homes than the next two largest [metros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_municipality_(South_Africa) \"Metropolitan municipality (South Africa)\") combined, and 152% more affordable homes than [Johannesburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannesburg \"Johannesburg\") (a significantly larger city by population) handed over during the same period.[[254]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_leads_metro_housing_race-256)\n\nIn early 2025, 14% of homes listed for sale were new to the market. The most common size of properties for sale was 2-bedroom, followed by 3-bedroom properties.[[235]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_Property_Trends_and_Statistics-237)\n\nThe city is home to some of South Africa's most expensive neighbourhoods, including [Constantia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantia,_Cape_Town \"Constantia, Cape Town\") and [Bishopscourt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishopscourt,_Cape_Town \"Bishopscourt, Cape Town\") in the [Southern Suburbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Suburbs,_Cape_Town \"Southern Suburbs, Cape Town\"); [Tamboerskloof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamboerskloof \"Tamboerskloof\") and [Higgovale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higgovale,_Cape_Town \"Higgovale, Cape Town\") in the [City Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Bowl \"City Bowl\"); and [Fresnaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresnaye,_Cape_Town \"Fresnaye, Cape Town\"), [Camps Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camps_Bay \"Camps Bay\"), [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Cape_Town \"Clifton, Cape Town\"), [Llandudno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llandudno \"Llandudno\"), and [Bantry Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantry_Bay,_Cape_Town \"Bantry Bay, Cape Town\") in the [Atlantic Seaboard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Seaboard,_Cape_Town \"Atlantic Seaboard, Cape Town\"). Affluent areas are popular with international buyers from countries including the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom \"United Kingdom\"), the [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands \"Netherlands\"), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany \"Germany\"), and [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France \"France\"), with some purchasing homes around R60 million (~ £2.5 million / €3 million / US$3.3 million).[[255]](#cite_note-Clifton_Remains_Cape_Town's_Most_Expensive_Suburb_to_Buy_Property-257)[[248]](#cite_note-Super_luxury_home-sales_boom,_Joburg_buyers_pay_more_for_Cape_Town_houses-250)[[250]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_foreign_buyer_hotspots_and_top_prices_paid_for_luxury_homes-252) 9 out of South Africa's 10 most expensive suburbs are in Cape Town.[[256]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_tops_R21billion_in_transactions-258)\n\nIn 2021, Cape Town's [residential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential \"Residential\") property market was valued at R1.22 trillion, the highest out of all major South African [metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_municipality_(South_Africa) \"Metropolitan municipality (South Africa)\"). The city's property market constituted 72% of the entire Western Cape's residential property market value, and 21% of the national property market value. The city had significantly more homes valued in the luxury market segment (at 43% of the total) than in any other major South African metro area. In the same year, there were around 800,000 residential properties located in Cape Town. 76% of those were detached homes, 15% were apartments, and 9% were semi-detached homes.[[257]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_Housing_Market_Report_-_2021-259)\n\nAs of April 2025, Cape Town had a home ownership rate of 6.6% among those aged 18 to 35; 22.7% among those aged 36 to 49; 35.7% for those aged 50 to 64; and 35% for those aged 65 or older. In the same month, 25.8% of new home buyers in Cape Town were aged 18 to 35.[[235]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_Property_Trends_and_Statistics-237)\n\n### Rentals\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=47 \"Edit section: Rentals\")]\n\nIn 2025, the average rent price in Cape Town for a 2-bedroom apartment was approximately [R](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_rand \"South African rand\")12,000 per month. However, this value varies significantly between suburbs. For a property of the same size, some neighbourhoods offer rent as low as R7,000 per month, while in other, more desirable areas, this may be as high as R30,000 per month. The city has excellent gross rental yields, with some properties generating as much as a 10.5% return on investment for owners.[[258]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_Rental_Market_in_2025:_Key_Trends_&_Insights_for_Landlords_&_Tenants-260)[[259]](#cite_note-Demand_for_SA_rental_property_on_the_rise-261)\n\nRental prices for apartments in affluent areas can often exceed the city's average by multiples. For example, in 2024, three areas in the Atlantic Seaboard – Camps Bay, Clifton, and Bantry Bay – averaged rental prices of R40,000 per month for a 1-bedroom apartment, and R120,000 per month for a 3-bedroom apartment. In Cape Town, popular rental website Property24 lists almost 100 rentals per month at prices over R100,000 per month, and some rentals cost as much as R300,000 per month.[[260]](#cite_note-These_Cape_Town_suburbs_charge_an_average_of_R120_000_for_rent-262)\n\nAverage rent prices in Cape Town increased by 5.2% in 2025, in line with the national average.[[261]](#cite_note-Fibonacci_development_set_to_attract_property_investors_and_to_aid_CPT_rental_shortage-263) As of 2024, the city has the lowest [rental vacancy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rental_vacancy_rate \"Rental vacancy rate\") out of all major South African metropolitan areas.[[260]](#cite_note-These_Cape_Town_suburbs_charge_an_average_of_R120_000_for_rent-262)\n\nIn 2021, approximately 18% of Cape Town residents rented their homes.[[257]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_Housing_Market_Report_-_2021-259)\n\nWith nearly 5,000 students from over 100 countries studying at the [University of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Cape_Town \"University of Cape Town\") (UCT), 18% of the student body is international, which increases demand for rental properties in Cape Town. Student housing – accommodation provided by the University of Cape Town – has a shortfall, which is made up by the private rental market. In 2023, there were 28,000 registered UCT students, with 8,400 of them securing UCT residence places.[[261]](#cite_note-Fibonacci_development_set_to_attract_property_investors_and_to_aid_CPT_rental_shortage-263)\n\nAs of 2025, rental prices in the Western Cape were the highest in South Africa, and 23% above the national average. It has been the most expensive province in South Africa for renting since 2016.[[262]](#cite_note-Dangerous_proposal_for_properties_in_Cape_Town-264)[[263]](#cite_note-One_South_African_province_where_rental_prices_shot_up-265) The Western Cape's reputation as South Africa's best-run province, with efficient governance and high-quality, well-maintained infrastructure, has made it an attractive destination for tenants.[[261]](#cite_note-Fibonacci_development_set_to_attract_property_investors_and_to_aid_CPT_rental_shortage-263)\n\nThe average rent price in the Western Cape in the fourth quarter of 2024 was R11,141. This was the first time the figure had gone beyond the R11,000 mark. The Western Cape also has the lowest number of tenants in arrears (those who are behind on rental payments) out of all South African provinces.[[264]](#cite_note-New_record_for_the_average_rent_paid_in_South_Africa-266) The Western Cape had a record-low vacancy rate of 1.07% in the third quarter of 2024.[[261]](#cite_note-Fibonacci_development_set_to_attract_property_investors_and_to_aid_CPT_rental_shortage-263)\n\n## Infrastructure and services\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=48 \"Edit section: Infrastructure and services\")]\n\nThe City of Cape Town has a strong focus and high level of spending on [infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_infrastructure \"Public infrastructure\") development and maintenance. The City spent a total of [R](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_rand \"South African rand\")9.5 billion on infrastructure development during the 2024 to 2025 financial year.[[265]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_sets_all-time_record_for_infrastructure_spending-267) For the 2025 to 2026 financial year, the city's budget was R12.6 billion. Its total spend ended up being R12.24 billion – the highest single year infrastructure investment spend for a city in South Africa's history.[[266]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_Mayor_announces_SA-record_infrastructure_investment-268) In the same year, the City apportioned R120 billion for its 10-year infrastructure portfolio.[[22]](#cite_note-2025_to_2026_Budget_-_Annexure_A-24) Cape Town has a larger infrastructure budget than the combined budgets of all three [metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_municipality_(South_Africa) \"Metropolitan municipality (South Africa)\") in the province of [Gauteng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauteng \"Gauteng\").[[22]](#cite_note-2025_to_2026_Budget_-_Annexure_A-24)\n\nMost goods are handled through the [Port of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Cape_Town \"Port of Cape Town\") or [Cape Town International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_International_Airport \"Cape Town International Airport\"). Most major shipbuilding companies have offices in Cape Town.[[267]](#cite_note-269) The province is also a centre of energy development for the country, with the existing [Koeberg nuclear power station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koeberg_nuclear_power_station \"Koeberg nuclear power station\") providing energy for the Western Cape's needs.[[268]](#cite_note-270)\n\nCape Town has five major [commercial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce \"Commerce\") nodes, with the city's [CBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_CBD \"Cape Town CBD\") containing around 40% of its office space.[[269]](#cite_note-State_of_Cape_Town_Central_City_Report_2022-271) [Century City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Century_City_(Cape_Town) \"Century City (Cape Town)\"), the [Bellville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellville,_Western_Cape \"Bellville, Western Cape\")/Tygervalley strip, Westlake, and [Claremont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claremont,_Cape_Town \"Claremont, Cape Town\") commercial nodes are well-established and contain many offices and corporate [headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headquarters \"Headquarters\"), for both local and international companies.\n\nCape Town's [property investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_investment \"Property investment\") and [construction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction \"Construction\") markets consistently perform well above its metropolitan peers in South Africa.[[188]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_hot_spot_for_building_opportunities,_Gauteng_market_softening_–_WBHO-190)\n\nIn September 2025, the City implemented new planning rules for property development that reduce approval times. Through its Municipal Planning Amendment by-law, costs for developers, and by extension buyers, are reduced. Furthermore, opportunities opened up for small-scale developers to build up to 12 [affordable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_housing \"Affordable housing\") rental units on certain residential plots, without the need to go through a [rezoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rezoning \"Rezoning\") process, thereby speeding up the building of [affordable housing in Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_housing_in_South_Africa#Cape_Town \"Affordable housing in South Africa\"). The City stated that it was also investigating the use of [artificial intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence \"Artificial intelligence\") (AI) to process future development applications.[[270]](#cite_note-New_property_rules_for_Cape_Town_to_speed_up_planning_approvals-272)\n\n### Telecommunications\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=49 \"Edit section: Telecommunications\")]\n\nCape Town has a well-established telecoms infrastructure network, which includes:\n\n* [Fibre to the premises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiber_to_the_x \"Fiber to the x\") for residential and commercial buildings. Fibre network operators (FNOs) like [Vumatel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vumatel \"Vumatel\"), Frogfoot, and [Octotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octotel \"Octotel\"), provide connectivity as high as 1 Gbit/s, with a separate 1 Gbit/s redundant line, to homes and business premises. These services also provide optional [VoIP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VoIP \"VoIP\") connectivity.[[271]](#cite_note-Vumatel_beats_Openserve_in_close_fibre_race-273)\n  + The city derives its main [undersea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undersea_cable \"Undersea cable\") connectivity from [West African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Africa \"West Africa\") cable routes, such as WACS, Equiano, and SACS, as well as Pan African cables like 2africa. Many of these land at either [Yzerfontein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yzerfontein \"Yzerfontein\"), around 2 hours from Cape Town, or [Melkbosstrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melkbosstrand \"Melkbosstrand\"), just to the north of the city.[[272]](#cite_note-All_the_undersea_cables_connecting_South_Africa_to_the_rest_of_the_world-274)\n* [5G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5G \"5G\") and [4G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4G \"4G\") [cellular networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellular_network \"Cellular network\"), with coverage in almost all areas of the metro region, from major providers such as [Vodacom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vodacom \"Vodacom\"), [MTN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTN_Group \"MTN Group\"), and [Cell C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_C \"Cell C\"), as well as options from [MVNOs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MVNO \"MVNO\") like [Rain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_(telecommunications) \"Rain (telecommunications)\").[[273]](#cite_note-Best_mobile_network_in_Cape_Town_revealed-275)\n\n### Health\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=50 \"Edit section: Health\")]\n\nThe city contains many well-equipped clinics and hospitals in both the public and private healthcare sectors.\n\nSee also: [List of hospitals in South Africa § Cape Metropole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_South_Africa#Cape_Metropole \"List of hospitals in South Africa\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Groote_Schuur_Hospital%2C_Observatory%2C_Cape_Town%2C_Western_Cape._05.JPG/250px-Groote_Schuur_Hospital%2C_Observatory%2C_Cape_Town%2C_Western_Cape._05.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Groote_Schuur_Hospital,_Observatory,_Cape_Town,_Western_Cape._05.JPG)\n\n[Groote Schuur Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groote_Schuur_Hospital \"Groote Schuur Hospital\")\n\n* The [Alexandra Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandra_Hospital_(Cape_Town) \"Alexandra Hospital (Cape Town)\") is a specialist mental health care hospital in Cape Town. It provides care for complex mental health issues and intellectual disability.[[274]](#cite_note-276)\n* [Groote Schuur Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groote_Schuur_Hospital \"Groote Schuur Hospital\") is a large, government-funded, teaching hospital situated on the slopes of Devil's Peak. It was founded in 1938 and is famous for being the institution where the first human-to-human heart transplant took place. Groote Schuur is the chief academic hospital of the University of Cape Town's medical school, providing tertiary care and instruction in all the major branches of medicine. The hospital underwent major extension in 1984 when two new wings were added.\n* Helderberg Hospital is a district hospital for the Helderberg basin. Located in Somerset West, it also serves surrounding areas in the Overberg district.\n\nNumerous of South Africa's major private medical services groups operate [hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_hospital \"Private hospital\") in the city, including those below.\n\n* [Mediclinic Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediclinic_Group \"Mediclinic Group\"): 7 facilities (Cape Town, Milnerton, Louis Leipoldt, Constantiaberg, Cape Gate, Durbanville, and Vergelegen)[[275]](#cite_note-Hospitals_&_Day_Clinics-277)\n* [Netcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netcare \"Netcare\"): 4 facilities (Christiaan Barnard, UCT Private Academic, Blaawberg, and N1 City)[[276]](#cite_note-Netcare_Smart_Search-278)\n* [Melomed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melomed \"Melomed\"): 5 facilities (Gatesville, Bellville, Tokai, Mitchells Plain, and Claremont)[[277]](#cite_note-Melomed-279)\n* [Life Healthcare Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_Healthcare_Group \"Life Healthcare Group\"): 3 facilities (Kingsbury, Vincent Pallotti, and Sports Science)[[278]](#cite_note-Life_Healthcare_-_Hospitals_in_Cape_Town-280)\n\n### Education\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=51 \"Edit section: Education\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/UCT_Upper_Campus_landscape_view.jpg/250px-UCT_Upper_Campus_landscape_view.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:UCT_Upper_Campus_landscape_view.jpg)\n\nThe [University of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Cape_Town \"University of Cape Town\")'s Upper Campus, in front of [Devil's Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devil's_Peak_(Cape_Town) \"Devil's Peak (Cape Town)\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/CPUT_Cape_Town_Campus.jpg/250px-CPUT_Cape_Town_Campus.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CPUT_Cape_Town_Campus.jpg)\n\nThe Cape Town Campus of the [Cape Peninsula University of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Peninsula_University_of_Technology \"Cape Peninsula University of Technology\"), with [Table Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_Mountain \"Table Mountain\") in the background\n\nPublic primary and secondary schools in Cape Town are run by the [Western Cape Education Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cape_Education_Department \"Western Cape Education Department\"). This provincial department is divided into seven districts; four of these are \"Metropole\" districts – Metropole Central, North, South, and East – which cover various areas of the metropolis.[[279]](#cite_note-281) There are also many private schools, both religious and secular. Cape Town has a well-developed higher system of [public universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_university \"Public university\").\n\nCape Town is served by three public universities: the [University of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Cape_Town \"University of Cape Town\") (UCT), the [University of the Western Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Western_Cape \"University of the Western Cape\") (UWC) and the [Cape Peninsula University of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Peninsula_University_of_Technology \"Cape Peninsula University of Technology\") (CPUT). [Stellenbosch University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellenbosch_University \"Stellenbosch University\"), while not based in the metropolitan area itself, has its main campus and administrative section 50 kilometers from Cape Town, and has additional campuses, such as the Tygerberg Faculty of Medicine and Health Sciences and the Bellville Business Park, north-west of the city in the town of [Bellville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellville,_South_Africa \"Bellville, South Africa\").\n\nBoth the University of Cape Town and Stellenbosch University are leading universities in South Africa. This is due in large part to substantial financial contributions made to these institutions by both the public and private sector.\n\nUCT is an [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language \"English language\") tuition institution. It has over 21,000 students and has an MBA program that was ranked 51st by the Financial Times in 2006.[[280]](#cite_note-282)\n\nIt is also the top-ranked university in Africa, being the only African university to make the world's Top 200 university list at number 146.[[281]](#cite_note-283) UCT has its main campus on the mountainside in [Rondebosch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rondebosch \"Rondebosch\"), a predominantly residential suburb in the [Southern Suburbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Suburbs,_Cape_Town \"Southern Suburbs, Cape Town\") region of Cape Town. The university also operates satellite campuses in [Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observatory,_Cape_Town \"Observatory, Cape Town\") and [Cape Town CBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_CBD \"Cape Town CBD\").\n\nSince the [African National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_National_Congress \"African National Congress\") has become the country's ruling party, some restructuring of Western Cape universities has taken place and as such, traditionally non-white universities have seen increased financing, which has evidently benefitted UWC.[[282]](#cite_note-284)[[283]](#cite_note-285)\n\nThe Cape Peninsula University of Technology was formed on 1 January 2005, when two separate institutions – [Cape Technikon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Technikon \"Cape Technikon\") and [Peninsula Technikon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsula_Technikon \"Peninsula Technikon\") – were merged. The new university offers education primarily in English, although one may take courses in any of South Africa's official languages. The institution generally awards the [National Diploma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Diploma_(South_Africa) \"National Diploma (South Africa)\").\n\nStudents from the universities and high schools are involved in the South African [SEDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEDS \"SEDS\"), Students for the Exploration and Development of Space. This is the South African SEDS, and there are many SEDS branches in other countries, preparing enthusiastic students and young professionals for the growing Space industry.[[284]](#cite_note-286)\n\nAs well as the universities, there are also several [colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College \"College\") in and around Cape Town. Including the [College of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Cape_Town \"College of Cape Town\"), False Bay College and Northlink College. Many students use NSFAS funding to help pay for tertiary education at these TVET colleges.[[285]](#cite_note-287) Cape Town has also become a popular [study abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Study_abroad_in_the_United_States \"Study abroad in the United States\") destination for many international college students. Many [study abroad providers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Study_abroad_organization \"Study abroad organization\") offer semester, summer, short-term, and internship programs in partnership with Cape Town universities as a chance for international students to gain intercultural understanding.\n\n### Safety and security\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=52 \"Edit section: Safety and security\")]\n\nCape Town has multiple [law enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement \"Law enforcement\") agencies working together to ensure the [laws of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_South_Africa \"Law of South Africa\"), as well as the provincial and municipal [bylaws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bylaws \"Bylaws\"), are upheld throughout the city. Different agencies have different jurisdictions, granted to them by various levels of government. In recent years, the City of Cape Town and [Western Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cape \"Western Cape\") province have been pushing to gain more independence from the [South African Police Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Police_Service \"South African Police Service\"), and to instead have full control of their [policing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Policing \"Policing\").[[286]](#cite_note-WC_crime_statistics_prompt_calls_for_devolution_of_policing_powers-288)[[287]](#cite_note-City_of_Cape_Town_pushes_for_control_over_police_investigations-289)\n\nIn recent years, the city has taken a focused approach towards combatting crime, investing in technological interventions such as dash and body cameras, CCTV, and drones.[[288]](#cite_note-City_boosts_its_tech_investment_for_safety-290) The Western Cape's Department of Police Oversight and Community Safety had an annual budget in 2024 of around R800 million, a large portion of which goes to Cape Town. Likewise, the City of Cape Town's Safety and Security Directorate has a healthy annual budget, which totaled around half a billion rand for the 2024 fiscal year.[[288]](#cite_note-City_boosts_its_tech_investment_for_safety-290)[[289]](#cite_note-MEC_Reagen_Allen_tables_Western_Cape_Police_Oversight_and_Community_Safety_budget_2024-291)\n\nCape Town's law enforcement is overseen by the city's Mayco Member for Safety and Security, the Western Cape Community Safety and Police Oversight [MEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Council_(South_Africa) \"Executive Council (South Africa)\"), and the Western Cape Police Commissioner.[[290]](#cite_note-City_of_Cape_Town_welcomes_113_new_metro_and_traffic_officers-292)[[291]](#cite_note-Law_enforcement_fighting_tooth_and_nail_to_rid_Cape_Town_of_crime_stigma-293)\n\nThe agencies working to ensure safety and security in the City of Cape Town are:\n\n#### The Cape Town Metropolitan Police Department (Metro Police)\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=53 \"Edit section: The Cape Town Metropolitan Police Department (Metro Police)\")]\n\nSee also: [Municipal Police (South Africa)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_Police_(South_Africa) \"Municipal Police (South Africa)\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Cape_Town_Metro_officers_and_car.jpg/250px-Cape_Town_Metro_officers_and_car.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cape_Town_Metro_officers_and_car.jpg)\n\nCape Town Metro Police officers and a Metro Police vehicle\n\nThe Metro Police are responsible for crime prevention, as well as the enforcement of municipal bylaws and traffic laws, conducting autonomous drug- and alcohol-related operations, executing warrants in support of the [South African Police Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Police_Service \"South African Police Service\"), and conducting visible policing in specific areas. Its special units include:\n\n* Gang and Drug Task Team\n* Tactical Response Unit\n* Canine Unit\n* Equestrian Unit\n* Camera Response Unit\n* Video Unit\n* CCTV Unit\n* Social Crime Prevention Unit[[292]](#cite_note-Metropolitan_Police_Services_Department-294)\n\nIn mid-2025, Cape Town rolled out its Neighbourhood Safety Officer (NSO) program. Part of the Metro Police, NSOs are officers dedicated to patrolling specific neighbourhoods across the city. On top of other kinds of law enforcement, the program positions 5 dedicated NSOs per City of Cape Town municipal ward.[[293]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_mayor_shows_off_700_new_officers_who_will_be_hitting_the_streets_to_keep_the_city_safe-295)\n\nThe Metro Police make use of vehicles including the [Toyota Corolla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Corolla \"Toyota Corolla\") for general law enforcement, the [Volkswagen Golf GTI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen_Golf \"Volkswagen Golf\") for the Flying Squad, and the [Toyota Fortuner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Fortuner \"Toyota Fortuner\") for the Highway Patrol.\n\n#### Traffic Service\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=54 \"Edit section: Traffic Service\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Police_of_South_Africa.jpg/250px-Police_of_South_Africa.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Police_of_South_Africa.jpg)\n\nCape Town Traffic Service officer on a motorbike in the [V&A Waterfront](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V&A_Waterfront \"V&A Waterfront\")\n\nThe city's Traffic Service has dedicated officers for improving road safety by providing effective [driving license](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driving_license \"Driving license\") services and [traffic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic \"Traffic\") enforcement. The Service employs specially trained peace officers.\n\nWhile Metro Police can and do also provide traffic-related enforcement, the Traffic Service focuses entirely on such. Cape Town's Traffic Service enforces traffic laws, ensures [roadworthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roadworthy \"Roadworthy\") compliance of all [motor vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_vehicles \"Motor vehicles\"), and provides driving license services. Matters it deals with include [drunk driving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drunk_driving \"Drunk driving\") offenses, vehicle impounds, animal impounds, displaced people, [graffiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graffiti \"Graffiti\") enforcement, informal trading, [liquor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquor \"Liquor\") enforcement, metal theft, [rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway \"Railway\") enforcement, and neighbourhood safety.[[294]](#cite_note-Law_Enforcement,_Traffic_and_Coordination_Department-296)\n\nTraffic Services began using dashboard-mounted cameras (dashcams) in 2023, for added security. Cape Town's Traffic Services operates a specialized Highway Patrol unit, with its own fleet of vehicles that are geared towards maintaining safety on the highways passing through the city.[[295]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_Law_Enforcement_Becomes_SA's_First_to_Use_Camera_Tech-297)\n\n#### Law Enforcement Advancement Plan (LEAP)\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=55 \"Edit section: Law Enforcement Advancement Plan (LEAP)\")]\n\nLEAP officers are specifically deployed in high-crime areas. They undergo separate training, and exist as an entirely separate police force to Metro Police. However, they work closely with Metro Police, as well as SAPS.\n\nLEAP officers deal with matters including the confiscation of illegal [firearms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firearms \"Firearms\"), violence prevention, [drug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug \"Drug\") and liquor confiscations, and anti-[gang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang \"Gang\") activities.[[296]](#cite_note-Law_Enforcement_Advancement_Plan_(LEAP)-298)\n\nThe City of Cape Town partnered with the Western Cape Government to launch LEAP in 2020, as part of the latter's Western Cape Safety Plan (WCSP), which was launched in September 2019.[[297]](#cite_note-MEC_Reagen_Allen_on_training_of_Law_Enforcement_Advancement_Plan_(LEAP)_officers-299)\n\n#### The South African Police Service (SAPS)\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=56 \"Edit section: The South African Police Service (SAPS)\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Police_at_Houses_of_Parliament.jpg/250px-Police_at_Houses_of_Parliament.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Police_at_Houses_of_Parliament.jpg)\n\nSAPS vehicles outside the main entrance to [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_South_Africa \"Parliament of South Africa\"), in the center of Cape Town CBD\n\n[SAPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Police_Service \"South African Police Service\") is the national policing authority in [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa \"South Africa\"). As such, they have a presence in Cape Town, as with all other major [metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_municipality_(South_Africa) \"Metropolitan municipality (South Africa)\"). Their jurisdiction covers the investigation of crimes (which may be handed over to them by the Metro Police). SAPS employs detectives, criminologists, forensic pathologists, and other related experts, as is governed by the [Department of Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Police_(South_Africa) \"Department of Police (South Africa)\") in the national government.[[298]](#cite_note-SAPS_-_Home-300)\n\nSAPS, along with Metro Police members, are held accountable by the [Independent Police Investigative Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Police_Investigative_Directorate \"Independent Police Investigative Directorate\"), which investigates wrongdoing within the police service across South Africa.\n\nIn the City of Cape Town, SAPS makes use of vehicles including the [Toyota Hilux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Hilux \"Toyota Hilux\"), [Toyota Fortuner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Fortuner \"Toyota Fortuner\"), and [Nissan Navara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nissan_Navara \"Nissan Navara\").\n\nIn early 2025, the [City of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Cape_Town \"City of Cape Town\") announced its intention to merge numerous police forces info a single law enforcement agency.[[299]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_to_merge_traffic,_law_enforcement_and_metro_police_into_single_force-301)\n\n#### Improvement Districts\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=57 \"Edit section: Improvement Districts\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/CCID_trailer%2C_guard_and_manager%2C_Cape_Town..JPG/250px-CCID_trailer%2C_guard_and_manager%2C_Cape_Town..JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CCID_trailer,_guard_and_manager,_Cape_Town..JPG)\n\nA CCID trailer, guard and manager in the city centre providing additional security to the local area.[[300]](#cite_note-CCID_-_Safety_and_Security-302)\n\nRatepayers in Cape Town fund a number [Business Improvement Districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_improvement_district \"Business improvement district\") (BID) across the city with the assistance of the City of Cape Town. As of 2025 there were 21 BIDs[[301]](#cite_note-303) funded by local ratepayers across the city to provide localised supplementary urban management, communications, and social development services in their respective areas.[[302]](#cite_note-:03-304)\n\nThe largest and oldest such district is the Cape Town [Central City Improvement District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_CCID \"Cape Town CCID\") (CCID), founded in 2000. CCID focuses on a roughly 1.6 sqkm area in [Cape Town CBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_CBD \"Cape Town CBD\"), the city's main economic hub.[[303]](#cite_note-About_the_CCID-305) The CCID also employs 12 dedicated Law Enforcement officers to assist with arrests. It is able to obtain access to the City of Cape Town's Strategic Surveillance Unit (SSU) CCTV cameras, to assist with crime prevention efforts.[[300]](#cite_note-CCID_-_Safety_and_Security-302)\n\nThe presence of BIDs like the CCID has been credited with significantly contributing to the general safety and prosperity of the city.[[300]](#cite_note-CCID_-_Safety_and_Security-302)[[304]](#cite_note-306)[[305]](#cite_note-307)[[306]](#cite_note-308)\n\n#### Law Enforcement Auxiliary Service (LEAS)\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=58 \"Edit section: Law Enforcement Auxiliary Service (LEAS)\")]\n\nCape Town's Law Enforcement Auxiliary Service (LEAS) has been operating since 2013, as part of the city's overall public safety program. LEAS comprises around 300 individuals, who volunteer for a minimum of 16 hours per month. LEAS volunteers operate under the supervision of permanent law enforcement officers. Uniformed LEAS officers assist with duties including patrolling (visible policing), enforcing municipal bylaws, and directing traffic. Non-uniformed officers assist with administrative tasks relating to law enforcement.[[307]](#cite_note-Volunteer_with_the_Law_Enforcement_Auxiliary_Service-309)\n\nLEAS officers undergo thorough vetting, are divided up between the 4 geographical areas within the metro, and are supported by an Auxiliary Animal Control Unit, Marine Unit, and Rural Safety Unit.[[308]](#cite_note-Volunteers_dedicate_nearly_70_000_hours_towards_a_safer_city-310)\n\n#### Other security measures\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=59 \"Edit section: Other security measures\")]\n\nThe City of Cape Town's Strategic Surveillance Unit (SSU), uses a system of around 2,000 [closed circuit television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closed_circuit_television \"Closed circuit television\") (CCTV) [cameras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameras \"Cameras\")[[309]](#cite_note-311) throughout the metropolitan area, monitoring suburbs for any suspicious activity, and using CCTV footage in the prosecution of crimes. The CCTV network is used by, among other agencies, Metro Police, LEAP, and SAPS (including for investigations). [Control room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_room \"Control room\") staff for the CCTV network are able to rapidly deploy response teams, ranging from police to emergency services, based on the events occurring. The cameras are therefore useful not only for crime prevention, but also general [public safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_safety \"Public safety\").[[310]](#cite_note-312)[[288]](#cite_note-City_boosts_its_tech_investment_for_safety-290)\n\nCCTV camera budgeting falls under the allocations of the Safety and Security Directorate, which also deploys communications equipment for various Cape Town neighbourhood watch organizations. The city's CCTV Cameras are also used for license plate recognition, by means of Automated Number Plate Recognition (ANPR) software. This is used by units including the city's Highway Patrol. In 2019, the City spent around R20 million on upgrading this system and purchasing new security vehicles.[[311]](#cite_note-City_of_Cape_Town_to_add_dozens_of_CCTV_cameras_to_surveillance_network-313)[[288]](#cite_note-City_boosts_its_tech_investment_for_safety-290)\n\nSince 2024, Cape Town has also made use of its Eye in the Sky (Eye) Information, Surveillance, and Reconnaissance initiative – an advanced, [drone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unmanned_aerial_vehicle \"Unmanned aerial vehicle\")-based aerial technology system designed to provide real-time monitoring of activities on the ground. Forming part of a broader push to integrate surveillance technologies into public safety and urban governance, Eye's unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) have been specially designed for fighting crime, and are considerably advanced as a result.[[312]](#cite_note-Watching_from_above:_Surveillance_in_Cape_Town-314)\n\nThe drones are equipped with infrared cameras, and can detect body heat in cold water, trace residual heat from discharged firearms, and capture the thermal signatures of high-speed vehicles. Furthermore, the drones can fly at very high altitudes, operate for long periods of time, and function in adverse weather conditions.[[312]](#cite_note-Watching_from_above:_Surveillance_in_Cape_Town-314)\n\nThe City uses similar drones for environmental issues, such as mountain fires and illegal marine poaching. Cape Town's Eye drones deliver high-resolution, real-time intelligence to the city's security agencies and emergency response teams, forming an integral part of the city's overall safety and security operations.\n\nOther forms of technology added recently to the city's safety and security arsenal include dashboard-mounted Automated Number Plate Recognition cameras, bodycams for police officers, gunshot detection systems for remote alerts, and handheld digital devices which are linked to the city's Emergency Policing Incident Control (EPIC) system, for fast-tracked incident logging, issuing of fines, and uploading of photographic evidence. Cape Town continues to modernize its policing systems, and in recent years, has spent over R600 million on technological advancements in the security space.[[288]](#cite_note-City_boosts_its_tech_investment_for_safety-290)\n\nMany Cape Town residents have access to additional security in the form of private security subscriptions, from the likes of large companies (with national reach), such as [Fidelity ADT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidelity_ADT \"Fidelity ADT\"). These companies provide [armed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firearm \"Firearm\") response teams for [residential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential \"Residential\") crime reduction, with private security personnel and vehicles also being stationed around Cape Town neighbourhoods for visible, localized security. ADT also partners with major South African fibre network operator (FNO) [Vumatel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vumatel \"Vumatel\"), to monitor areas via CCTV, and some suburbs' residents have arranged for cameras to be installed around their houses for 24/7 surveillance, to increase local security.\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Private_Security_Vehicles_in_Meadowridge%2C_Cape_Town.jpg/250px-Private_Security_Vehicles_in_Meadowridge%2C_Cape_Town.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Private_Security_Vehicles_in_Meadowridge,_Cape_Town.jpg)\n\nPrivate security vehicles in [Meadowridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meadowridge,_Cape_Town \"Meadowridge, Cape Town\"), Cape Town\n\nThe city is part of the Public Emergency Call Center (107) network, for crime reporting and response.[[313]](#cite_note-Alderman_JP_Smith:_Mayoral_Committee_Member_for_Safety_and_Security-315)\n\nCape Town has been a pioneer with policing and security innovations in South Africa. The City runs the country's only police reservist service that is managed by a local government, in the form of the Disaster Volunteers, and the Law Enforcement Auxiliary Service. It was also first to launch specialized policing units including the Gang and Drug Task Team, and the Metal Theft Unit.[[313]](#cite_note-Alderman_JP_Smith:_Mayoral_Committee_Member_for_Safety_and_Security-315)\n\nThe city is also the only metropolitan area in South Africa to use gunfire detection systems in high-risk areas, and to effectively implement computer-aided dispatch and integrated spatially enabled resource management through its award-winning EPIC system. Furthermore, Cape Town employs the largest CCTV network out of all South African cities.[[313]](#cite_note-Alderman_JP_Smith:_Mayoral_Committee_Member_for_Safety_and_Security-315)\n\n### Energy supply\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=60 \"Edit section: Energy supply\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Steenbras_upper_and_lower_dams.jpg/250px-Steenbras_upper_and_lower_dams.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Steenbras_upper_and_lower_dams.jpg)\n\nThe [Steenbras Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steenbras_Power_Station \"Steenbras Power Station\"), which is used to generate hydroelectric power for the city\n\nAs with many other forms of infrastructure, Cape Town stands out amongst South Africa's metropolitan areas for having a far more developed, stable [energy supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_generation \"Electricity generation\"). The city is home to the country's only nuclear power station - [Koeberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koeberg_Nuclear_Power_Station \"Koeberg Nuclear Power Station\") – as well as numerous other forms of power generating plants. A second nuclear station, situated in Duynefontein (slightly south-east of Koeberg) is planned. The new plant received Environmental Authorization in 2025, and awaits further statutory approvals by the national government.[[314]](#cite_note-Government_upholds_Eskom's_approval_to_build_new_nuclear_power_station-316)\n\nFurthermore, Cape Town uses a system of pumped hydro electricity storage (at [Steenbras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steenbras_Power_Station \"Steenbras Power Station\")) to supplement its own power generation and avoid up to 2 stages of [load shedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Load_shedding \"Load shedding\") (power outages), when the rest of the national grid goes down due to [insufficient energy to meet demand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_energy_crisis \"South African energy crisis\"). This results in not only residential homes having more sustained power feeds, but also allows commercial and industrial operations to continue when other major areas in the country are without power, boosting Cape Town's economic output.\n\n#### Energy infrastructure developments\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=61 \"Edit section: Energy infrastructure developments\")]\n\nIn January 2024, it was reported that the City of Cape Town intended to leave national energy operator [Eskom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eskom \"Eskom\") behind, and become completely energy self-sustaining. Through adding 650MW of independent power to the city's grid, Cape Town aims to protect itself against the first 4 stages of Eskom load-shedding by 2026, with the goal of eventually becoming entirely self-sufficient, meeting its own electricity demand. The closing date for tender submissions for this project was 8 April 2024.[[315]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_plan_to_leave_Eskom_behind-317)\n\nThe City aims to procure power from existing generators, and will follow a two-pronged structure, allowing bidders to supplement dispatchable or reserve power with an optional self-dispatchable component – the latter being at a lower cost than the prevailing Eskom Megaflex Tariff.[[315]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_plan_to_leave_Eskom_behind-317)\n\nCape Town was able to go ahead with this plan after successful advocacy to the national government for the integration of independent power producers (IPPs) into the national grid. The [City of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Cape_Town \"City of Cape Town\") took the Department of Mineral Resources and Energy (DMRE) and the National Energy Regulator of South Africa (Nersa) to court, after Nersa said it could not allow for independent power production unless the Ministry made changes.[[316]](#cite_note-ER-318)\n\nCape Town's progress with a holistic energy plan, detached from Eskom, was achieved following the gazetting of amendments to the electricity regulations for new electricity generation capacity in 2020, by then-Mineral Resources and Energy Minister Gwede Mantashe.[[316]](#cite_note-ER-318)\n\nThe new regulations opened the way for municipalities in good financial standing to develop their own power generation projects. President Ramaphosa announced the changes in a speech to Parliament in the same year, which marked the first time municipalities were allowed to buy energy from sources other than state-owned Eskom's monopolistic system.[[316]](#cite_note-ER-318)\n\nRecent and in-progress power diversification methods include:[[315]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_plan_to_leave_Eskom_behind-317)\n\n* Private Small-Scale Embedded Generation (SSEG), providing up to 100 MW\n* Embedded independent power producer [renewable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy \"Renewable energy\"), providing up to 200MW\n* A dispatchable IPP Program, providing up to 500MW\n* [Wheeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheeling_(electric_power_transmission) \"Wheeling (electric power transmission)\"), providing up to 350MW\n* City-owned SSEG, providing up to 20MW\n\nThe City of Cape Town announced a \"Cash for Power\" program in 2024, as part of its overall Energy Strategy. The program allows for Cape Town households to earn money for selling excess power they generate via solar panels back to the city's grid – a first for South Africa. The City opened applications for residential households to feed power into the grid by means of grid-tied solar photovoltaic (PV) systems and bi-directional meters.[[317]](#cite_note-CoCT_launches_Cash_for_Power_programme_alongside_Energy_Strategy_unveiling-319)\n\nDuring the announcement speech, [Cape Town's Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Cape_Town \"Mayor of Cape Town\") also criticized the [national government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_South_Africa \"Government of South Africa\") and [Eskom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eskom \"Eskom\") for not fixing the [load shedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Load_shedding \"Load shedding\") problems in South Africa, and said that Cape Town would not wait for matters with the national grid to be fixed, with the [City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Cape_Town \"City of Cape Town\") instead making its own power generation plans.[[317]](#cite_note-CoCT_launches_Cash_for_Power_programme_alongside_Energy_Strategy_unveiling-319)\n\nCape Town has South Africa's most advanced plans to buy power on the open market. In February 2025, the city announced that its Cash for Power program had 1,842 active participants, and had paid out over R55 million to its residents and businesses as part of the program, via credits applied to their municipal energy bills. 1,090 small-scale generators (sellers) were residential customers, and 752 were commercial or industrial. The city also stated that it was on track to double the payouts in the program's second year of operation.[[318]](#cite_note-Cash_for_Power:_Capetonians_reach_R50m_earnings_mark-320)\n\nThe City further stated that it had launched an online system for registering solar PV systems for the program, and announced unveiled a cheaper, bi-directional meter to feed power back into the grid. Customers can offset their electricity bills without registering for the Cash for Power program. However, registering allows them to go into a credit status, with the City paying them back for any excess power generation.[[318]](#cite_note-Cash_for_Power:_Capetonians_reach_R50m_earnings_mark-320)\n\nIn 2025, the conclusion of a year-long electricity wheeling pilot project was announced. 562,800 kWh of renewable electricity was successfully wheeled during the pilot. Enpower Trading, Etana Energy, and [Equites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equites_(company) \"Equites (company)\") were the traders for the process. Cape Town Mayor [Geordin Hill-Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geordin_Hill-Lewis \"Geordin Hill-Lewis\") announced that the city would enter the next phase of the project, whereby it would promote the scaling up of power trading across Cape Town's electricity grid, based on bilateral and multilateral trading agreements.[[319]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_moves_to_open_electricity_grid_to_traders_after_pilot-321)\n\nThe Mayor described the milestone as an exciting leap forward, to diversify Cape Town's electricity supplier base beyond Eskom, and towards a future of decentralized electricity trading in South Africa.[[319]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_moves_to_open_electricity_grid_to_traders_after_pilot-321)\n\nIn June 2025, the City of Cape Town's Atlantis solar PV plant had finished the installation of around 2,400 solar panels out of a planned 12,850 panels, as part of a R200 million renewable-energy project. City of Cape Town Energy MEC Xanthea Limberg stated that the Atlantis site would operate as a hybrid plant, alongside the city's first utility-scale battery storage operation, totaling 8 MW. There are plans for a 60 MW solar plant to be built at Paardevlei, which is expected to provide sufficient protection from one stage of Eskom load shedding.[[320]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_own-build_solar_plant_on_track_for_year-end_completion-322)\n\nCape Town has a goal of net-zero carbon municipal buildings by 2030, as well as a goal for alternative energy to meet 35% of the city's total demand by 2030.[[320]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_own-build_solar_plant_on_track_for_year-end_completion-322)\n\nThe city's changes to its power infrastructure represent major changes in South Africa's overall electricity network, including a shift towards green energy, the integration of wheeling processes, a move towards energy diversification as a reaction to rising Eskom tariffs, and shorter-term, flexible contracts in a merchant market providing businesses with easier methods to source stable power.[[321]](#cite_note-Beyond_load_shedding:_4_key_energy_trends_reshaping_SA_in_2025-323)\n\nIn February 2026, [Cape Town Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_Mayor \"Cape Town Mayor\") [Geordin Hill-Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geordin_Hill-Lewis \"Geordin Hill-Lewis\") announced that the [City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Cape_Town \"City of Cape Town\") would that same month begin the process of engaging with a private electricity trading company to provide Cape Town with additional energy. The agreement would significantly shift how the city sources its power, breaking the monopoly long-held by national producer Eskom, and allowing Cape Town to appoint a trading company to purchase power from a range of private energy providers.\n\nThe City has already entered into numerous supply agreements with various companies that would, following the appointment of the trading company, build the required generation capacity. The Mayor said that using a trading platform alleviates the need to negotiate private power agreements contract by contract directly with providers, streamlining the energy sourcing process for the City.\n\nEnergy sourced via the new trading method and private providers is set to be cheaper than that sourced from Eskom, and will also mostly be derived from [renewable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy \"Renewable energy\") sources, in keeping with Cape Town's overarching net-zero carbon emission goals.[[322]](#cite_note-Major_South_African_city_dumping_Eskom-324)\n\nIn September 2026, Cape Town became the first city in South Africa to sign a [power purchase agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_purchase_agreement \"Power purchase agreement\") with an [independent power producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_power_producer \"Independent power producer\") (IPP), allowing the municipality to source energy outside of [Eskom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eskom \"Eskom\"), and at competitive rates. This was possible due to the recent [liberalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalization \"Liberalization\") of South Africa's [energy market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_South_Africa \"Electricity sector in South Africa\"), which was historically controlled exclusively by [state-owned enterprise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprise \"State-owned enterprise\") Eskom.[[323]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_seals_first_agreements_to_buy_power_at_below_Eskom_rates-325)\n\nThe [City of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Cape_Town \"City of Cape Town\") signed two [solar energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_energy \"Solar energy\") purchasing agreements, worth a combined 70MW, and has plans to procure a further 130MW, for a total of 200MW under the current tendering process. The City said this would prevent unexpected price hikes to consumers, as the private energy increases would be linked to the [Consumer Price Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_price_index_in_South_Africa \"Consumer price index in South Africa\") (CPI) each year. The energy procured from the new IPPs came in at around 20% cheaper than Eskom purchasing rates.[[323]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_seals_first_agreements_to_buy_power_at_below_Eskom_rates-325)\n\n### Water supply\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=62 \"Edit section: Water supply\")]\n\nMain article: [Western Cape Water Supply System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cape_Water_Supply_System \"Western Cape Water Supply System\")\n\nCape Town's water is supplied via the **Western Cape Water Supply System** (WCWSS), a complex water supply system in the [Western Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cape \"Western Cape\") province. The system comprises an interlinked network of six main dams, as well as pipelines, tunnels, distribution networks, and a number of minor dams - some owned and operated by the national [Department of Water and Sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Water_and_Sanitation \"Department of Water and Sanitation\"), and some by the [City of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Cape_Town \"City of Cape Town\") [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_municipality_(South_Africa) \"Metropolitan municipality (South Africa)\") government.[[324]](#cite_note-Kasrils-326)\n\nCape Town has extremely high-quality drinking water, ranking the best in South Africa as of February 2026, according to [Stats SA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stats_SA \"Stats SA\").[[325]](#cite_note-City_of_Cape_Town_Water_Supply_-_Stats_SA-327)\n\n#### Water crisis of 2017 to 2018\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=63 \"Edit section: Water crisis of 2017 to 2018\")]\n\nMain article: [Cape Town water crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_water_crisis \"Cape Town water crisis\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Water_Crisis.jpg/250px-Water_Crisis.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Water_Crisis.jpg)\n\nWater crisis warning in Cape Town in 2018\n\nThe Cape Town water crisis of 2017 to 2018 was a period of severe water shortage in the [Western Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cape \"Western Cape\") region, most notably affecting the [City of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Cape_Town \"City of Cape Town\"). While dam water levels had been declining since 2015, the Cape Town water crisis peaked during mid-2017 to mid-2018 when water levels hovered between 15 and 30 percent of total dam capacity.\n\nIn late 2017, there were first mentions of plans for \"Day Zero\", a shorthand reference for the day when the water level of the major dams supplying the city could fall below 13.5 percent.[[326]](#cite_note-USA_today-328)[[327]](#cite_note-Globe_and_Mail-329)[[328]](#cite_note-Poplak_2018-330) \"Day Zero\" would mark the start of Level 7 water restrictions, when municipal water supplies would be largely switched off and it was envisioned that residents could have to queue for their daily ration of water. If this had occurred, it would have made the City of Cape Town the first major city in the world to run out of water.[[329]](#cite_note-Booysen_et_al_2019-331)[[330]](#cite_note-Booysen_et_al_2019a-332)\n\nThe city of Cape Town implemented significant water restrictions in a bid to curb water usage, and succeeded in reducing its daily water usage by more than half to around 500 million litres (130,000,000 US gal) per day in March 2018.[[331]](#cite_note-Narrandes_2018-333) The fall in water usage led the city to postpone its estimate for \"Day Zero\", and strong rains starting in June 2018 led to dam levels recovering.[[332]](#cite_note-Myburgh_2018-334)\n\nIn September 2018, with dam levels close to 70 percent, the city began easing water restrictions, indicating that the worst of the water crisis was over.[[333]](#cite_note-reduce-sep2018-335) Good rains in 2020 effectively broke the drought and resulting water shortage when dam levels reached 95 percent.[[334]](#cite_note-After_drought-336) Concerns have been raised, however, that unsustainble demand and limited water supply could result in future drought events.[[335]](#cite_note-337)\n\n#### Water infrastructure developments\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=64 \"Edit section: Water infrastructure developments\")]\n\nThe City of Cape Town has mapped out a New Water Program, and Water Strategy, which plan to add 300 million litres of water per day to the city's supply, through measures including desalination, tapping into groundwater, and clearing invasive species.[[336]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_to_wrap_up_desalination_plant_feasibility_study_in_2025,_first_water_possible_in_2030-338)\n\nIn mid-2024, the Cape Town municipality stated that it was embarking on an infrastructure stability program, which involves refurbishing infrastructure and building new capacity, with funds for the program already having been added to the city's budget.[[337]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_needs_to_spend_well_over_R2_billion_to_achieve_water_security_in_the_next_decade-339)\n\nThe City of Cape Town announced in 2024 that it planned to build a desalination plant, to further strengthen Cape Town's water supply. A feasibility study was planned to be completed in 2025. The facility is expected to produce between 50 million and 70 million litres of water a day, with a targeted starting date of 2030.[[336]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_to_wrap_up_desalination_plant_feasibility_study_in_2025,_first_water_possible_in_2030-338)\n\nThe plant will be located on undeveloped land, at the intersection of the R27 and N1, in the vicinity of the [Port of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Cape_Town \"Port of Cape Town\"). The city is undertaking an environmental impact assessment process for the project.[[337]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_needs_to_spend_well_over_R2_billion_to_achieve_water_security_in_the_next_decade-339)\n\nCity of Cape Town Water and Sanitation MMC Zahid Badroodien stated that the proposed Paarden Island desalination plant would feature a 22,000 square meter sea water, reverse-osmosis operation, for use by Cape Town households. The estimated capital cost of the plant was roughly R5 billion, excluding VAT, and will be funded through the city's water tariff.[[336]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_to_wrap_up_desalination_plant_feasibility_study_in_2025,_first_water_possible_in_2030-338)\n\n### Future infrastructure developments\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=65 \"Edit section: Future infrastructure developments\")]\n\nAs part of Cape Town's Vision 2050 project, the City aims to significantly upgrade its infrastructure. The 25-year project, unveiled by the City in mid-2025, was opened for public comment for a period of around 2 months. At the time, Cape Town's Mayor stated that the project's overarching goal was for Cape Town to remain a, \"future-fit city of opportunities for all\". 75% of the project's goals will directly benefit lower-income households.[[338]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_unveils_ambitious_25-year_vision-340)\n\nSome of Vision 2050's aims include:[[338]](#cite_note-Cape_Town_unveils_ambitious_25-year_vision-340)[[339]](#cite_note-Cape_Town's_Vision_2050:_Mayor_calls_for_comment_on_the_City's_Long-Term_Plan-341)\n\n* Public transport accounting for 75% of all trips, with no resident spending more than 10% of their income on commuting.\n* 35% of electricity obtained from alternative sources by 2030, supported by grid upgrades.\n* 25% of water obtained from non-surface sources by 2040.\n* 50,000 new housing opportunities created annually, through both land releases and private-sector partnerships.\n* Taking over passenger rail from the national government, to revive it as the city's transport backbone.\n* Expanding the city's policing powers to combat crime more effectively. This includes Smart Policing that is data and tech-driven.\n* Diverting 70% of waste from landfills through methods including recycling and better waste management.\n* Increased use of digital technology and artificial intelligence for better governance and improved communication with residents.\n* Improved disaster risk capacity to deal with climate challenges.\n* Biodiversity protections, and increasing annual tree planting rates to plant 100,000 trees by 2050. This is in line with the city's Urban Forest Policy.\n* A 120% increase in the city's gross economic output by 2050.\n\n## Transport\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=66 \"Edit section: Transport\")]\n\n### Air\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=67 \"Edit section: Air\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Airlink_at_Cape_Town_International_Airport.jpg/250px-Airlink_at_Cape_Town_International_Airport.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Airlink_at_Cape_Town_International_Airport.jpg)\n\n[Cape Town International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_International_Airport \"Cape Town International Airport\")'s terminal building, with [Airlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airlink \"Airlink\") planes in front\n\n[Cape Town International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_International_Airport \"Cape Town International Airport\") serves both domestic and international flights.[[340]](#cite_note-342) It is the second-largest airport in South Africa and serves as a major gateway for travellers to the Cape region. Cape Town has regularly scheduled services to other parts of [Southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa \"Southern Africa\"), [East Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Africa \"East Africa\"), [Mauritius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauritius \"Mauritius\"), [Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East \"Middle East\"), [Far East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_East \"Far East\"), [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe \"Europe\"), [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil \"Brazil\") and the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States \"United States\") as well as eleven domestic destinations.[[341]](#cite_note-343)\n\nAs tourism numbers increased in the lead-up to the tournament of the [2010 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_FIFA_World_Cup \"2010 FIFA World Cup\"), Cape Town International Airport opened a brand new central terminal building that was developed to handle an expected increase in air traffic.[[342]](#cite_note-344)\n\nOther renovations include several large new parking garages, a revamped domestic departure terminal, a new Bus Rapid Transit system station and a new double-decker road system. The airport's cargo facilities are also being expanded and several large empty plots are being developed into [office space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office \"Office\") and hotels.\n\nCape Town is one of five internationally recognised [Antarctic gateway cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic_gateway_cities \"Antarctic gateway cities\") with transportation connections. Since 2021, commercial flights have operated from Cape Town to [Wolf's Fang Runway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfs_Fang_Runway \"Wolfs Fang Runway\"), Antarctica.[[343]](#cite_note-345) The Cape Town International Airport was among the winners of the World Travel Awards for being Africa's leading airport.[[344]](#cite_note-346) Cape Town International Airport is located 18 km from the [Central Business District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Business_District \"Central Business District\").[[345]](#cite_note-347)\n\nA second airport has been proposed to serve Cape Town. As of 2025, [Cape Winelands Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Winelands_Airport \"Cape Winelands Airport\"), currently a private airfield, is undergoing planning for redevelopment. It is situated near [Durbanville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durbanville \"Durbanville\"), in Cape Town's [Northern Suburbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Suburbs,_Cape_Town \"Northern Suburbs, Cape Town\"). If approved and completed, it will become the city's first private airport, and aims to focus on domestic travel, increase Cape Town's global competitiveness as a travel destination, and serve as a backup aviation hub for Cape Town International.[[346]](#cite_note-Cape_Winelands_Airport_awaits_decision_on_environmental_assessment_to_begin_construction-348)\n\nCWA's redevelopment is financially backed by South Africa's largest [real estate investment trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_estate_investment_trust \"Real estate investment trust\") (REIT), [Growthpoint Properties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growthpoint_Properties \"Growthpoint Properties\"), and the chosen construction contractor is the country's largest of its kind, [WBHO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WBHO \"WBHO\"). The airport aims to be operational by early 2028, and accommodate over 5 million passengers annually by 2050.[[347]](#cite_note-New_Cape_Winelands_Airport_a_step_closer_to_taking_off-349)\n\n### Sea\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=68 \"Edit section: Sea\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Port_of_Cape_Town.jpg/250px-Port_of_Cape_Town.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Port_of_Cape_Town.jpg)\n\nThe [Port of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Cape_Town \"Port of Cape Town\") is a major transport node in [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa \"Africa\"). In addition to moving freight, it also hosts cruise ships, and serves as a major repair site for both ships and oil rigs\n\nCape Town has a long tradition as a port city, and its role as a re-provisioning stop at the midpoint of the [Cape Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Route \"Cape Route\") gained it the nicknames \"Tavern of the Seas\" and \"Tavern of the Indian Ocean\".[[348]](#cite_note-350) The [Port of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Cape_Town \"Port of Cape Town\"), the city's main port, is in Table Bay directly to the north of the CBD.\n\nThe port is a hub for ships in the southern Atlantic: it is located along one of the busiest shipping corridors in the world, and acts as a stopover point for goods en route to or from Latin America and Asia. It is also an entry point into the South African market.[[349]](#cite_note-EU_Handbook_2020-351) It is the second-busiest container port in South Africa after [Durban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durban \"Durban\"). In 2004, it handled 3,161 ships and 9.2 million tonnes of cargo.[[350]](#cite_note-352)\n\n[Simon's Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon's_Town \"Simon's Town\") Harbour on the False Bay coast of the Cape Peninsula is the main operational base of the [South African Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Navy \"South African Navy\").\n\nUntil the 1970s the city was served by the [Union Castle Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Castle_Line \"Union Castle Line\") with service to the United Kingdom and St Helena.[[351]](#cite_note-353) The [RMS *St Helena*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RMS_St_Helena_(1989) \"RMS St Helena (1989)\") provided passenger and cargo service between Cape Town and [St Helena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Helena \"St Helena\") until the opening of [St Helena Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Helena_Airport \"St Helena Airport\").[[352]](#cite_note-354)\n\nThe cargo vessel M/V *Helena*, under AW Shipping Management, takes a limited number of passengers,[[353]](#cite_note-Cargoships-355) between Cape Town and St Helena and [Ascension Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_Island \"Ascension Island\") on its voyages.[[354]](#cite_note-356) Multiple vessels also take passengers to and from [Tristan da Cunha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tristan_da_Cunha \"Tristan da Cunha\"), inaccessible by aircraft, to and from Cape Town.[[355]](#cite_note-357) In addition, NSB Niederelbe Schiffahrtsgesellschaft takes passengers on its cargo service to the [Canary Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canary_Islands \"Canary Islands\") and [Hamburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburg \"Hamburg\"), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany \"Germany\").[[353]](#cite_note-Cargoships-355)\n\n### Rail\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=69 \"Edit section: Rail\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/X%27trapolis_Mega_on_Kalk_Bay_bridge_Dec_2023.jpg/250px-X%27trapolis_Mega_on_Kalk_Bay_bridge_Dec_2023.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:X'trapolis_Mega_on_Kalk_Bay_bridge_Dec_2023.jpg)\n\n[Metrorail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrorail_(Western_Cape) \"Metrorail (Western Cape)\") train near [Kalk Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalk_Bay \"Kalk Bay\") station\n\nThe [Shosholoza Meyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shosholoza_Meyl \"Shosholoza Meyl\") is the passenger rail operations of the [Passenger Rail Agency of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger_Rail_Agency_of_South_Africa \"Passenger Rail Agency of South Africa\") and operates one long-distance passenger rail service from Cape Town as of 2024: a weekly service to and from [Johannesburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannesburg \"Johannesburg\") via [Kimberley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberley,_South_Africa \"Kimberley, South Africa\"). These trains terminate at [Cape Town railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_railway_station \"Cape Town railway station\") and make a stop at [Bellville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellville_railway_station \"Bellville railway station\"). Cape Town is the terminus for two luxury tourist train routes as of 2024 operated by the Ceres Rail Company, traveling from the [Waterfront](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V&A_Waterfront \"V&A Waterfront\") to [Simon's Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon's_Town \"Simon's Town\") and [Grabouw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grabouw \"Grabouw\") respectively.\n\n[Metrorail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrorail_(Western_Cape) \"Metrorail (Western Cape)\") operates a [commuter rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commuter_rail \"Commuter rail\") service in Cape Town and the surrounding area. The Metrorail network consists of 96 stations throughout the suburbs and outskirts of Cape Town.\n\n### Road\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Cape_Town&action=edit&section=70 \"Edit section: Road\")]\n\nCape Town is the origin of three [national routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_routes_in_South_Africa \"National routes in South Africa\") (\"N\" routes), namely the N1, N2, and N7.\n\nThe [N1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N1_(South_Africa) \"N1 (South Africa)\") begins at the foreshore area near the Cape Town city centre and runs east-north-east as a freeway through the towns of [Goodwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodwood,_Western_Cape \"Goodwood, Western Cape\"), [Parow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parow,_South_Africa \"Parow, South Africa\"), [Bellville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellville,_Cape_Town \"Bellville, Cape Town\"), [Brackenfell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brackenfell,_Cape_Town \"Brackenfell, Cape Town\") and [Kraaifontein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraaifontein \"Kraaifontein\") before continuing towards [Paarl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paarl \"Paarl\"). It connects Cape Town to major cities further inland, namely [Bloemfontein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloemfontein \"Bloemfontein\"), [Johannesburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannesburg \"Johannesburg\"), and [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria \"Pretoria\").[[356]](#cite_note-:02-358) An older at-grade road, the [R101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R101_road_(South_Africa) \"R101 road (South Africa)\"), runs parallel to the N1 from Bellville to [Worcester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worcester,_South_Africa
